Dear Mr. Dickover,
I hear from the Foreign Office that
among German Consular archives taken over
in Lourenço Marques were some letters in
the Japanese language addressed to various
894.414/7-1645
Japanese nationals. Our respective Consuls
General in Lourenço Marques have agreed that
these letters shall be forwarded to Washington
for examination. We should be glad, however,
4R800.414
if you could turn them over to us afterwards
as we should like to have a look at them.
Yours sincerely,
Mneon
F. C. Everson.
Mr. Erle R. Dickover,

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
FOREIGN ACTIVITY CORRELATION
Dc/k
November 2, 1945
JA - Mr. Turner:
In view of the unimportant nature of the information con-
tained in the Japanese letters received with despatch No. 556,
FW 894.414/7-1645
July 30, 1945, from Lourenço Marques, according to the summaries
attached hereto, FC is not particularly interested in their dis-
position. However, it is suggested that in compliance with
the British request as indicated in your memorandum of October 22,
1945 they be given the originals and a copy of the summary, with-
out a statement indicating further disposition. In this manner,
we will always have the right to ask for their return should
such a course later be found necessary.

STANDARD FORM NO. G4
TO
: FC : R. E. Carroll
DATE: 10-22-45
FROM : JA : WiIliam T. Turner
SUBJECT:
V-21945
Reference 1s made to a menorandum from Mr. Oscar S.
Strauss, dated August 29, 1945, transmitting despatch No.
556 from Lourenço Marques dated July 30, 1945 accompanied
by a number of letters in the Japanese language.
FW 894.414/7-1645
There are enclosed three copies of a report prepared
in this division summarizing the contents of these letters.
Although it was originally intended to prepare complete
translations of each letter, examination revealed that some
of the longest letters were written by Japanese women and
were concerned practically exclusively with femily matters.
The material is therefore oresented in the form of trans-
lation of extracts, with emphasis being given to the mention
of the movements of individuals within Japen's former
"Co-Prosperity Sphere". Practically all the letters are
dated in August, 1942.
When you have examined the report, it would be appre-
ciated if you would indicate what disposition should ulti-
mately be msde of the original letters. In a letter of
July 16, l945, to Mr. Erle R. Dickover from Mr. F.C. Everson
of the British Embassy in Washington the latter said in re-
gard to this material, "We should be glad...if you would
turn them over to us afterwards, as we should like to have
a look at them." The question arises whether we should ask
Mr. Everson to re urn
the letters to us after examination,
or whether he may dispose of them as he sees fit.

November 7, 1945
In reply refer to
JA
My dear Mr. Everson:
Reference 1s made to your letter of July 16, 1945
to Mr. Erle R. Diokover of this Department in regara
to certain letters in the Japanese language whish were
found among the arohives of the former German Cemsulate
General at Lourengo Marques, Portuguese East Africa.
The letters were received in the Department in the
early part of September and have been examined in detail.
894.414
They are herewith enelosed together with a summary or the
contents prepared in the Department. Since nearly all of
them are dated in August, 1942, the information obtaimed
from them 1s now probably of little value. It may be,
hovever, that some of this material may prove of assistanes
in identifying individuals who were engaged in Japem's
program for the development of "Greater East Asia".
/7-1645
Sincerely yours,
81945
William T. Turner
Chief
Division of Japanese Affaire

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
WAR PROBLEMS
SPECIAL/DIVISION
August 17, 1945
SECRET
TO: A-H - General Holmes
1. SUBJECT:
894.414/8-1745
Custody of Japanese diplomatic and consular property
and archives upon the relinquishment of such property
to the custody of the United States Government by the
Powers protecting Japanese interests in the United
States, i.e. the Swiss Government protecting Japanese
interests in the United States with the exception of
the Territory of Hawaii and the Swedish Government
protecting Japanese interests in the Territory of
Hawaii.
2. THE PROBLEM:
To attain the objective of maintaining Japanese offioial
property and archives as a pool of information available
to those agencies of the United States Government
legitimately interested and those members of the United
Nations which accord reciprocity.
3. BACKGROUND
The Swiss Government has been requested to transmit a
message to the Japanese Government according to which
the Japanese Government is instructed to authorize the
appropriate Governments protecting its interests to
relinquish diplomatic and consular property and
archives to the custody of Allied powers in belligerent
S
countries. It will be necessary that the Japanese
diplomatic and consular archives and property in the
United States relinquished in accordance with the fore-
going be appropriately controlled and made available by
the proper agency of the United States Government which,
as was considered appropriate with regard to similar
former German property and archives, is the Department
of State.
4. RECOMMENDATIONS:
(a) That the custody and responsibility for Japanese
diplomatic and consular property and archives and
any

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
2:4
any other comparable Japanese property which was
held with permission of this Government by the Swiss
or Swedish missions at Washington in oharge of
Japanese interests in the United States be placed in
the Department of State.
(b) That the Chief Special Agent make all arrangements to
take over physioal custody of such property and arehives
from the Swiss and Swedish missions immediately upon the
release thereof by the respective protecting powers.
The time and place of this transfer should ocour as mmy
be agreed upon between SWP and the Swiss and Swedish
missions at Washington. CSA and SWP should be
authorized to sign inventories of property thus de-
livered to the Department. SWP should be authorized
to verify and sign protocols relating to matters other
than deliveries of property.
(c) That all official Japanese archives, including reference
books, be centralized at Washington. Stored property
elsewhere to be reviewed and useless items disposed of.
In connection with (b) and this step, CSA should be
authorized to enlist the services of the FBI and other
expert assistance, retaining oustody for the Department,
however, of all items conoerned.
(d) That the Japanese diplomatic and consular arohives
so concentrated be made equally available to all agencies
of this Government legitimately interested and, subjeot
to reciprocity, to representatives of other governments.
As a matter of firm policy, no part of this material
should be removed from the premises upon which it is
concentrated except for a specified period of time and
against a responsibly signed receipt. In order to avoid
any occasion for the removal of any part of the arohives,
ample facilities including a photostat machine should be
made available for study and examination of the doouments
on the premises. Removal of property even though against
receipt should be primarily for the purpose of laboratory
eramination.
(e) That a professional and custodial staff be installed in
the Japanese Embassy building to arrange for the use of
the materials assembled there and to preserve them
against attempts toward diesipation.
(f) That SWP with the assistance of RP, FC, and CS▲ develop
a detailed plan for staffing the premises.
(g) That the foregoing program be initially financed out of
omergency funds as a number of governmental agencies can
be

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.94)
-3-
245
be expected to benefit from the program and since
its duration is indeterminate. A decision might be
made at a later time regarding the possibility of
requesting a special appropriation for the purpose.
(h) That SWp be charged with the responsibility of
coordinating and supervising the foregoing program.
5. CONCURRENCES:
FC Kon
CSAJ. J. J.

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
TO
: sWP -- Mr. Plitt
246
DATE: August 18, 1945
FROM : FE -- Mr. Ballantine
oument Must Be Returned to
Archives
AUG 2 01945
894.414/8
With reference to your memorandum of August 17, addressed
to General Holmes, containing recommendations for taking control
of Japanese property and archives in the United States, comment
is offered as follows:
We concur with SWP in regard to the desirability of making
available to agencies of this Government, and of cur Allies, in-
formation contained in the Japanese archives which might be of
value in connection with the implementation of the Potsdam
88
Declaration. We feel impelled to point out, however, that there
345
are certain considerations in regard to the legal situation which,
in our opinion, render it necessary to consider carefully the
question of methods to be adopted in attaining this objective.
P.W.C. 297-n approved by the Department several months ago
contains the following:
"American authorities should continue to respect Japanese
consular and diplomatic archives in the United States and its
territories and should continue to permit a neutral power,
selected by Japan, to protect such archives."
There have so far been no changes in conditions which, in
our opinion, should alter this recommendation. Even after the
surrender instrument has been signed the functions of the Govern-
S
ment in Japan have not been taken over by the Allied powers as
were those of Germany, but on the other hand the Japanese Govern-
ment is in existence and it will be permitted to continue to exist
under the terms of the Potsdam Declaration. Consequently our rights
in respect to Japanese diplomatic and consular archives are limited
to directing the Japanese Government to make those archives available
to us and to utilize them for the purpose of effectuating the terms
of surrender.
We believe that the proper procedure is therefore to direct
the Japanese Government, after the surrender instrument has been
signed, (see paragraph 3, reply to Japanese acceptance of Potsdam
Declaration, August 11, 1945) to request the protecting powers to
permit

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.98)
27
-2-
permit representatives of the Allied Governments to examine
diplomatic and consular archives in order to copy such material
as may be necessary for carrying out the Potsdam terms. In our
opinion archives or other property should not be removed from
the premises and should remain under the custody of protecting
powers subject to the above conditions.
The telegram received from our Embassy in Lisbon, August 16,
1945, indicates that the Portuguese Government takes the view
that the Japanese and German situations differ, and to refuses
to permit the Allied powers to take over the Japanese archives in
Portugal without the consent of the Japanese Government.
Even after Japan's surrender it is not clear what warrant
there is for directing the Japanese Government to take action
which is not necessary for carrying out the Potsdam Declaration.
For example: It is reasonable, after Japan's surrender, to call
upon the Japanese Government to make their archives available for
inspection for purposes of implementing the Potsdam terms and also
to instruct the protecting power to prevent destruction of archives,
but it is not clear what warrant there is for taking over property
of the Japanese Government and disposing of it as implied in the
second sentence of (c) of your recommendations.
In the light of the steps which this Government has already
taken (circular telegram of August 14, 1945 and telegram 2535 of
August 14, 1945 to Bern) it is recommended that we await the re-
actions of our Allies, of neutral Governments and of the Japanese
Government before determining precisely the next steps which we
should take in this matter.
In setting down the above considerations we are not so much
concerned with the interests of the Japanese Government as we are
with the honor and integrity of the American Government, which,
with our Allies, proposed the terms set down in the Potsdam
ultimatum, terms which have been accepted by the Japanese Govern-
ment to our material advantage - estimated by Mr. Churchill at
1,000,000 American lives - and which we are now in honor bound
to observe.

894.414/
I spoke yesterday with Max Wershoff apropos of
the Department's circular telegram August 29, 5 p.m.,
and told him of the War Department's instructions to
General MacArthur.
702.9400
894.00
He told me that shortly before the Japanese
6
surrendered the Spanish Government asked to be relieved
of their duties in connection with Japanese interests.
The Swiss were therefore asked by the Canadian Government
to take over, and although they appeared to approve they
4
never actually did take over. At the present time they
01
are not sure whether Switzerland desires to act or not.
In the meantime the Spanish Consul General is still act-
ing and is apparently willing to turn over the archives
to the Canadian Government should they so desire.
Wershoff said that the Department of External Affairs
disapproved taking custody and would await completion
of legal steps before doing so. He added that he doubted
very seriously if the archives would contain anything of
interest.
I believe my informal conversation with him served
to let him know that we were definitely interested in
getting the Japanese archives, and I believe that we can
rest assured that they will not be destroyed.

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.97)
CONFIDENTIAL
REPORT ON THE EXAMINATION OF CORRESPONDENCE
TAKEN FRON JAPANESE OFFICIAL PERSONNEL
CAPTURED IN GERMANY
Part I.
I. Scone of Examination
Up to the present time twenty-six files of correspondence
have been examined. These comprise about sixty letters written
by twenty-eight persons, the letters ranging in date from March
1941 to February 1945. Included in the files were also two
commercial reports by a Japanese economic observer, one naval
intelligenoe report (in triplioate), and one letter to a Jap-
anese technical speoialist from a German techniolan and written
in German. The correspondence files contain numerous photo-
89
graphs, ohiefly of children and family members. There are some
photographs showing the writers of the letters in Paris, the
4.414/9-445
south of France, northern Italy, et cetera. In one oase a
German officer friend of the writer appearing in the photograph
1s named.
In the great majority of inetances the addressees of the
letters are family members, prinoipally women. In a few instan-
ces the woman to whom the letter is addressed is obviously the
writer's wife. The examiner found it diffioult to asoertain
the exact relationship of the addressee in many instanoes since
he or she was addressed only by a given name. Some lotters are
addressed to fathers, aunts, uncles, and elder brothers, the
addressees then being specified as such.
The correspondence of three of the writers, consisting
of nine letters, were in a handwriting nearly iilegible to the
examiner, and so are not included in the survey. These nine
letters are not inoluded in the total of sixty letters examined.
II. Method
The letters were first read, and extensive notes taken on
them. In some cases considerable extracts were translated. This
was done to facilitate summarizing, since in the majority of

After an attempt to segregate the letters into the three
preceding attitudes on the war, the attempt was abandoned. The
majority of writers repeatedly demonstrated what might be termed
"determined pessimism'. Their attitude 1s best expressed by
the Japanese word "gambari", which, indeed, they frequently
used.
The examination of the correspondence was undertaken
primarily to determine attitudes, but it is believed that the
attitudes may best be illustrated by e series of quotations
appearing as Part II of this report.

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Iv. Ideology of the Writere
The political viewpoints expressed by the writers, when
expressed at all, were practically uniformly "orthodox Japanese".
In all the sixty letters examined, no indication was found
that any writer believed that Japan had foolishly embarked on
a hazardous course, or was in any way in the wrong in the present
war. One must, of course, bear in mind that the writers were
no doubt conscious of the Japanese censorship office. The
majority of the writers labor under a sense of impending
tragedy, but they feel that if there is any remedy for the
situation it is merely greater effort and sacrifice on the part
of the Japanese people. If there has been any cause for
possible failure it is merely that the effort and saorifice
have not been suffiolent.
Only three of the writers ofrer any extensive ideologloal
material of interest. Akira KATo makes intelligent observations
on the deterioration of Italian morale, and says, in March,
1944, "much can be learned from the present turmo1l in Italy."
Chugo KIMURA conoludes his rather routine observations in a
curious way, the last sentence of which seems to savor of
cynicism or perhaps it is just the habitual Japanese apologetie
courtesy which prompts him to suggest that he is boring his
reader. On November 25, 1n 1944(7) he writes:
"When I came to learn of the recent events in Formosa
and the Philippines, I shed tears. In this I am not
alone. All my countrymen feel the same way. The
Imperial Army's campaign, resolute, and carrying out
a defensive strategy difficult to endure for the last
three years, is divinely supported. Everyone in the
world must admit it. The hundred million people express
their gratitude to the offioere and men of the Imperial
Army far away. we, few though we be, must ae usual
hasten the viotory of the anoestral land, even though
in strange places. That spirit, that morale, will not
stop until Amerios and Britain, relying on waterial
things lie prostrate before the Spirit of Japan and
Yamato damashi1. The hundred million people, like iron,
forming a compact body of ammunition, will hasten for-
ward to take on the burden. This 1s a perquisite of
office; forgive me for having written bombast."
(Note: The writer says both "Spirit of Japan" literally,
and then adds the equivalent expression Yamato damashil.)

Masamoto KITAJIMA, on August 15, 1944 writes "the individ-

fall of the state" and adds that if Hitler had died in the
assassination attempt of June 30, Japan could no longer have
looked with admiration toward Germany. The writer holds that

foroes of German individualism in cheek" and he attributes

Mr. Kitajimas' views constitute the most extensive and syete-
matic 1deologioal exposition to be found in the correspondence.
As may be seen they "out-nazi the Nazis."
The examiner looked in vain through all the mixty letters
to find any viewpoint expressed that could be remotely inter-
preted as "liberal" or "democratio".

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.98)
Part III.
General Conolusion
Examination of their personal correspondence reveals that
the ma jority of the officially conneoted Japanese residing in
Germany and Axis ocoupied territory were reduced to a condition
of hysterics and heroics by the autumn and winter of 1944.
They probebly seoretly thought the war had already been lost.
They had no program for the future other than for each to mave
his own family and himself as best he could. It may be doubted
whether an examination of the remaining letters taken from
Japanese civilian personnel captured in Germany would alter
this conclusion.

RMH
Dear Carrigan:
I have before me you
to Sidney U'Donoghue referring to his telephone conversa
tion with Mrs. Hughes on August 28 regarding the matter
of Japanese property and archives. You confirm that the
Department feels it best to hold this matter in abeyance
pending developments in Japan: i.e., the signature of
the surrender document. I have noted with particular
interest that we are waitin until the surrender document
is signed, at which time we shall ask the War Department
894.414/9-845
to instruct General MacArthus to tell Hirohito to instruct
the Swiss to turn over to us the property in question.
of course, we must so through with this matter of
taking over the Japanese archives. So far as other
property is concerned, 1 do not believe there is any and
the information which we have is that there is no other
property.
I think we are treating the Japanese rather gently
in paying them the courtesy in waiting until after the
surrender documents are signed before taking over the
archives, but perhaps it is best even with the defeated
enemy to maintain the amenities although I confess that
so far as our defeated enemies in this war are concerned
there is no reason for observing any of the amenities.
The delay, I believe, does not make any difference
except that it must be embarrassine to some of our Chiefs
of
John W. Carrigan, Esquire,
Chief of Division of Mexican Affeirs,
Department of State,
Washington, D. C.
C
CC

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
2
AIR MAIL
-2-
of Mission. On the basis of the instructions which we had
received, we tot in touch with our Russian, British and
Chinese colleagues and in consequence thereof we had, for
instance here in Mexico City, a meeting of the four of us
in my office, concerning which we have reported. The
British Ambassador at the time had had no instructions but
telegraphed for them and cot them, and now he calls me up
every other day and I have told him that I have had to hold
matters so far as we are concerned until I received further
instructions. My Chinese colleague got his instructions
from his government changed so that he is at liberty to
take any action which I may inaicate I am instructed to
take. The instructions of my Soviet colleague so far are
to the effect that he is to collaborate with his colleagues,
that is, the British, Chinese and this Embassy, in asking
the Mexicans to take over the Japanese archives and make
them accessible to us - in other words, the same instruc-
tions that we had with regard to the German archives.
The Soviet has indicated that he wants to see me on Monday
and I am sure that it is to ask about this matter.
Althouch the British, Chinese, Soviet and this Embassy
are convinced that there is nothing in the official Japanese
archives in charge of the Swiss which will be of any interest,
the Soviet and Chinese Missions here seem to be very anxious
to do something about it. I am convinced that the Japanese
destroyed their archives even more fully and completely
than the Germans here did, so that the whole performance
will really be only a formal one but one which I think
should be gone through.
I do not know what the reaction of the other American
Covernments may be, but I am sure that so far as the Mexican
government is concerned there are only two things which we
have any hope of their doing. one is that we should ask
them - the four of us - as we aid in the case or the German
archives that they should take them over and make the contents
thereof available to us without necessarily asking for the
privilege of examination or the documents themselves. The
second is that we ask the Mexican government to take over
the documents and then make them available for examination
by all five of us here. I think the first procedure is the
logical, sound and surest one and I think it is the one
which

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
250
-3-
which we should follow in Mexico and in the other American
Republics. To try to insist that we have access to the
documents themselves, which is equivalent to their being
in the joint possession of these four powers and the
Mexicans, will not serve any real purpose as there is sure
to be nothing in the way of aocuments or anything in the
documents which exist which is of any use. I see, therefore,
no point in making an issue of this matter or getting into
any kind of a snarl over sovereignty or equality.
In this connection I might mention that I think the
Mexicans have made available to us everything that they
found in the German archives which was of any interest.
These code books were all that there was, and we had the
Civil Attaché photograph them and the photographs were
sent to Washington. Our Mexican friends have not failed
to indicate to us in a nice way on several occasions that
when we approached them on the question of the German
archives, it was indicated that we wanted to do it on an
exchange basis and that we would make available to them
information which we  ot from these archives just as they
would make available to us information which they got
from the archives here. In a very nice way they have
made it clear that we have not given them anything which
we may have secured from archives elsewhere, which I think
has been a nice way of saying that it has hardly been on
a reciprocal basis, but they have not maue a point of it
so far.
I am only mentioning this because I think we have to
bear it in mind in connection with the approach we make
on the Japanese archives.
I have some so far unsubstantiated but interesting
information that when the Japanese diplomats left here
they left with the Swedish inister their "archives",
which means what they aiu not burn or destroy, but that
they gave to Japanese living in Mexico City some aocuments
which they did not wish to destroy. We are keeping very
quiet about this information, but as soon as I get authority
to go aheau with my colleagues to approach the liexican
government with respect to the Japanese archives, I will
inform the Mexican government of this information which
we

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.98)
AIR MAIL
21
-4-
we have concernine these alleged other Japanese archives
in the possession of Japanese private citizens here, and
I am sure the Mexican government will lay hands on them
and make the contents thereof available to us if such
documents do exist. If they do exist, they will be, of
course, more interesting then any the Swedish Minister
may have.
I have written you at greater length than I intended
but I wanted to pull this matter together for your informa-
tion and that of the interested officers of the Department,
and I would appreciate your iving such distribution to
this letter as may seem useful.
With all good wishes,

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
Méxioo, D.F.,Móxico,
Septemb610, 1945.
MEMORANDUM
The Soviet Chargé d'Affaires ealled on me this
morning. He referred to the conference which we had
in the Embassy some time ago, attended by the British
Ambassador and myself and the Soviet and Chinese Chargé
d'Affaires, regarding Japanese archives. I told him that
after our meeting I had had instructions from my Govern-
ment to hold up any action on this matter, vis-a-vis, the
Mexican Government,and that I was still awaiting such
instructions. I said that I could only assume that there
was being further conversation between our respective
Governments on this matter and that as soon as I had any
definite instructions from my Government I would not fail
to get in touch with him. This seemed to satisfy him on
this point. He then spoke about German and Japanese
property in Mexico and said that he thought our respeotive
Governments would have to watoh very carefully what the
Mexican Government did about such property. I told him
so far as intervened property was conoerned of both
Germans and Japanese, we were following this matter very
closely in this Embassy. I said that it appeared that
the five principal powers had got together so far as German
reparations were concerned with respeet to European coun-
tries and that I had had an instruction to the effect that
the Latin American Republics would have to look to the
property within their own territories for reimbursement
for claims against the German Government and nationals.
It looked to me therefore as though it was going to be
entirely up to the Governments of the other American
Republics as to what action they would take with regard
to German property because on the action which they took
would depend their reimbursement for claims which as
Governments or as their citizens, they might have against
the German and Japanese Governments and nationals.
He asked some questions with regard to the intervened
Cerman and Japanese property and I told him that so far as
details were concerned, he could get in touch with Mr.
Lockett, the Economic Counselor of the Embassy.
GSM

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
FOREIGN ACTIVITY CORRELATION
September 11, 1945
Subject: Japanese diplomatic and consular
archives and property.
History
894.414/9-1145
On August 13, 1945, Assistant Secretary DUNN requested
Assistant Secretary HOLMLS to take action regarding the
Japanese diplomatic archives and property similar to that
taken with respect to German archives and property. After
General Holmes despatched the telegrams indicated below,
the matter was turned over to FC.
On August 14, 8 pm, a circular telegram (copy attached)
was sent to All American Diplomatic Missions instructing
them to request FONOFF and protecting powers to give custody
of archives and property to all missions acting on behalf of
USA, USSR, UK and China.
At the same time instructions were sent to Amembassy
Bern (DEPTEL 2535, attached) requesting the Swiss to communi-
cate a note to the Japanese GOVT instructing that GOVT to
surrender custody of the property and archives. To the note
the Japanese replied that the Potsdam declaration did not
provide for such measures.
Thereupon a message was sent to General MacArthur
C
requesting him to cause the Japanese to issue appropriate
S
instructions with regard to this matter. The SAC replied
that he did not feel that at that time he had the necessary
M
authority to enforce such an order.
Shortly thereafter instructions were sent to General
MacArthur requesting that he issue such orders when the
time was propitious.
DV181955
Problem
-1/45

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
Problem
A) Many of our missions have taken action and have
written notes to the FONOFFS and protecting powers requesting
custody of the archives and property. What is to be done
until neral MacArthur issues instructions - there may be a
delay of a week, a month, or a year before such instructions
are issued.
B) Is it contemplated that JAPAN is to have no representa-
tion abroad? If so, custody would be in order; if not, it
would appear that only temporary custody of archives would be
in order as the property would be needed by the Jap missions.
9
FC:OSStraus:EOA

: CON -- Mr. Lyon

suecr: Japanese diplomatic and consular
archives and property.
With reference to your memorandum of
September 12, 1945, on the subject of
Japanese diplomatic and consular archives
and property, and especially to questions
(A) and (B) propounded in the memorandum
appended thereto by Mr. Straus, I would
offer suggestions as follows.
With regard to (A), pending the issuance
of orders by General MacArthur to the Japanese
Government, referred to in the last paragraph
under the caption "History" in Mr. Straus'
memorandum, T see no alternative to leaving
the matter in abeyance until such orders are
issued. You might wish to consider, however,
sending a circular telegram to all diplomatic
missions to which the circular telegram of
August 14, 8 p.m. was sent, instructing them
to desist from further action on that circular
pending action by the Supreme Allied Commander
in Japan.
With regard to (B), so far as I am aware
no decision has been reached by this Govern-
ment as to whether Japan is to have any
diplomatic

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.98)
STANDARD FORM NO. 
DATE:
TO
FROM :
SUBJECT :
-2-
diplomatic representation abroad. It is
my understanding that the British Govern-
ment is inclined to favor taking steps to
remove Japan's diplomatic representation
abrcad. It may be that there will be a
decision to that effect, but on the other
hand it may be that we shall ask for the
recall of only certain Japanese diplomats
abroad. In any case, it is difficult to
see what action we can take toward even
temporary custody of Japanese archives
pending action by the Supreme Allied
Commander vis-a-vis the Japanese Government.

Port-au-Prince, Haiti, September 19,19l
AMILAN AFFAIRS

Subject:
Examination of Archives of Japanese Consulate
at Port-au-Prine
The Nonorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
894.414/8.294J
S1501.9438/s 18
In continuation of the Embassy's telegram no.
269 of Agust 18, 1 p.m., and with reference to the
Department's secret circular telegram/of August 29,
5 p.m., I have the honor to report that the archives
of Mr. Edouard Esteve, a Haitian citizen who was
Honorary Japanese Consul at Port-au-Prince, have been
examined by a Secretary and the Civil Attaché of this
Embassy for the period from 1935, when Mr. Esteve was
appointed Honorary Consul, to 1941 when he resigned.
Arrangements for the examination of these archives
were made some time ago with the Haitian Foreign Office
but aue to the fact that Mr. Esteve has been hospital-
ized in the United States for eighteen months, and has
not been well enough to attend to business since his
recent return to Port-au-Prince, it was not possible
actually to examine them until the last few days.
The archives consist principally in routine con-
sular correspondence, none of which was considered by
the Embassy to be worth photographing. However, it
is described in some detail below in order that the
Department may have an idea of the nature of the cor-
respondence.
Mr. Esteve, at the request of the Japanese Lega-
tion at Havana, habitually furnished monthly statistics
of Haiti's foreign trade with Japan, with the United
States, and with all other countries, the Japanese
FILED
Legation apparently being chiefly interested in the
Japanese competitive position vis-à-vis the United
States. The Japanese Legation also habitually received
copies of Le Moniteur, the Haitian official journal,
through the Honorary Consul here.
894.414
There was
DOR ARe Unit
lamaLe
a 2. 1970FB1
12.17176
R.v.
8kow
C11
OCT 1 RED

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
There was a fairly large volume of correspondence
from firms located in Japan and from Mr. K. Mituzani,
representative of the Federation of Japanese Exporters'
Associatios for Latin America, who was formerly station-
ed at Ciudad Trujillo, with regard to the sale of or
possibilities for sale of all kinds of Japanese goods,
including such diverse items as cotton piece goods,
porcelains, rubber goods, musical instruments, imitation
pearls, fishing nets, electrical goods, etc. Upon read-
ing over these files the impression is obtained that
Mr. Mituzani occupied a position more important than
that of an ordinary Chamber of Commerce representative,
as from his correspondence with Mr. Esteve it appears
that he had a good deal to do with the arrangements
for the visit of the Commercial Mission sent by the
Japanese Government to Haiti and other Latin American
countries in 1936. In connection with the visit of the
Japanese Commercial Mission, a letter dated February 10,
1936 from Takahari Ito, then Japanese Chargé d'Affaires
at Havana, to Mr. Esteve was found in which Ito insist-
ed that Haiti accept as a basis for negotiation of a
new commercial treaty importation of Japanese goods
valued at five times (or at a minimum four times) the
value of goods Haiti would be able to export to Japan.
Ito went on to inquire if Mr. Sidney de la Rue, the
Fiscal Representative, still insisted on a doliar for
dollar excnange as a basis for negotiation of the
projected commercial treaty between Haiti and Japan,
and threatened that the Japanese Commercial Mission
might not be able to visit Haiti if the Haitian Govern-
ment were not in a position to consider the Japanese
basis for negotiation. The actual figures proposed
were that Japan would export annually to Haiti goods
valued at $3,000,000 as against the importation into
Japan of Haitian goods valued at 600,0o0. It is pre-
sumed that the main idea on the part of the Japanese
was to assure the entry of Japanese cotton piece goods
into Haiti against exportation of Haitian coffee. The
Commercial Mission did visit Haiti, but according to
Mr. Esteve, the treaty was never negotiated, and the
Haitian Government was obliged to increase duties lo0%,
and in the case of silk 200%, to prevent Haiti from
being flooded with Japanese goods.
Other correspondence was found concerning a ship-
ment of Japanese films to Haiti for the purpose of
acquainting Haitians with Japanese trade resources and
possibilities, and concerning a shipment of Japanese
phonograph records to Haiti to be used for the same
purpose. This was the only correspondence relating to
what could be properly classified as propaganda material
that was found.
One of

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
One of the most interesting letters in these
files was one dated February 15, 1939 from K. Nakayama,
who was then in charge of the archives of the Japanese
Legation at Havana, asking for thirty models of Haitian
passports of all classes for the Japanese "Department
of Immigration to study and know their identity and to
avoid difficulties for entrance of foreigners into
Japan". Fortunately, the Haitian Governmsnt refused
to provide these sample passports, which the Japanese
could have easily used for fraudulent purposes, or
possibly in connection with espionage activities.
On May 22, 1942, Manuel Acal y Marin, Spanish
Minister at Ciudad Trujillo, requested Mr. Edouard
Esteve to turn over the archives of Japanese Consulate.
It will be recalled that this occurred immediately
after Mr. Esteve offered his resignation as Honorary
Japanese Consul on May 19, 194l. The request was
repeated in a letter from Daniel G. Marcos, Spanish
Vice Consul at Petit Goâve,under date of June 8, 1942,
who said he had been designated to take the archives
by the Spanish Minister at Ciudad Trujillo. Mr. Esteve
refused to turn over the archives on the ground that
he, a Haitian citizen, during the period when Haiti
was at war, could not deliver them to any one without
the authorization of the Haitian Foreign Office.
One other letter appears deserving of particular
mention. Iwai and Company, Ltd., 233 Broadway, New
York City, wrote Mr. Esteve on February 19, 1941,
asking if there were any restrictions in force on the
export of scrap iron and steel from Haiti to Japan, and,
according to Mr. Esteve, a certain quantity of scrap
was exported to Japan about that period.
There was quite a large volume of correspondence
between the Japanese Legation at Havana and Mr. Esteve
about the purcha se of champagne, which the Legation
apparently habitually procured in Haiti.
Mr. Esteve was compensated by the Japanese
Government for his duties as Honorary Consul at the
rate of Yen 200 per annum, or $99.70, which he received
in two semi-annual checks, and about which there was
a considerable volume of correspondence. It is the
impression of the ofricers reviewing the files that
the Yen 2o0 per annum was probably insufficient to
cover the cost of stationery, clerical assistance, etc.,
incidental to handling the correspondence.
The only publication found was one entitled "Regu-
lations and Instructions for the Information of Honorary
Consuls" published by the Commercial Bureau of the
Japanese Department for Foreign Affairs in March 1924.
The Civil

record of the names of all Japanese mentioned in Mr.
Esteve's files who formerly resided in the Caribbean
area, for possible future reference.
If the Department desires further information
with respect to this matter, the archives of the
Japanese Consulate at Port-au-Prince are available
to the Embassy for inspection at any time.
Respeotfully yours,
Wainwright Abbott

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.99)
I. SUMARY
For the preservation in Japan of information of political and
economic significance--for use in the administration of occupied areas,
settlement of property restitution questions, apprehension and prosecu-
tion of war criminals, and in carrying out military and industrial
disarmament--it is recommended that:
1. Military Government prohibit under severe penalties the removal,
mutilation or destruction of all records of public agencies, of quasi-
governmental or semi-public organizations, of political and "patriotic"
organizutions, and records which private organizations are required by
Japanese law to keep for business and other purposes.
2. As soon as possible after occupation of Japan, specially in-
structed officers of Military Government should take measures to safe-
guard by sealing, seizing and impounding, placing under military custody,
or by other security measures, the following records:
a. Certain records of the Cabinet Boards and Bureaus, the
Board of Audit, the Ministeries, Regional Councils, Prefectural
Governments, and other public agencies
b. Certain records of the quasi-governmental agencies--the
Control Socictics, Control Associations, Nutional Policy Companies,
Control Companies, and Eidans
c. Records of individual business enterprises engaged in war
production
d. Certain banking records--those of the contral banks, the
financial control societies, and banking institutions engaged in
financing war production and developnent of colonial and occupied
areas.
c. Certain records of underwriters of property insurance
f. Records of Japunese political and "patriotic" organizations
g. The records of such other organizations or enterprises as
may be taken over or closed by Military Government.
h. Catalogues, indices to files, and similar finding aids of
key records left under Japanese custody.
CUNFIDENTIAL

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
II. INTRODUCTION
A. Importance of Preserving Key Records
Records of the Japanese Government, the various quasi-governmental agencies,
and private companies and associations will be of vital importance to Military
Governnent and the Allied Powers for the following purposes:
1. In the adninistration of occupied areas--(i) records of agricultural
agencies which will give data on the productive capacity of certain
areas, where food is stored, and allocations of food for civilian
consumption; and (ii) records of agencies controlling industrial
production and aliocation of raw materials, which will supply infor-
mation concerning the location of stockpiles of raw materials and
semi-finished goods which may be utilized by Military Government in
reviving and controlling essential civilians goods industries;
2. In the ultimate settlement of property restitution questions--finan-
cial records establishing what property of Allied governments and
nationals was confiscated;
3. In the apprehension and prosecution of war criminals--records of the
former
governmental offices administering occupied areas and of the/Imperial
Rule Assistance Association and similar organizations, which will
identify individuals who are to be held responsible;
4. In carrying out military and industrial disarmament--records of the
industrial combines and corporations, the Finance, the Conmerce and
Industry and the Lunitions Linistries and the public and semi-public
agencies under their control, which will supply information concern-
ing war production, including civilian goods plants conerted for war
purposes.

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
B. Problems Confronting Military Government
Cabinet Ministries have had ample opportunity to destroy much or all of their
top secret documents and records. It is unlikely, however, even in the-Minis-
tries concerned with the prosecution of the war, the administration of occupied
areas, and control of war production, that all confidential records will have
been destroyed before occupation by Allied Military forces. In the prefectural
and local offices, headquarters of the eight military and administrative
regions, and war plants throughout Japan, where considerable confidential
information of military anu industrial value has been filed, there may have
been less systematic destruction of records.
Cn the other hand, it is possible that Allied occupation of the various
islunds and continental areas at present under Japanese control may not take
place as soon as that of the principul cities in Japan proper. In these out-
lying areas, the local officials will utilize such opportunity as they have to
destroy any evidence which will condemn them. It is, therefore, important
that such information concerning the administration of these areas as is
duplicated in the records of the Ministries und offions of ti Centrl aorn
ment be carufully safeguarded.

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
III GENERAL RECOMMENDATIONS CONCERNING METHODS OF SAFEGUARDING
In order to protect Japanese key records against deliberate or involuntary
destruction, and to facilitate their utilization for the purposes of Military
Government, the following steps are recommended for safeguarding them:
A. Sealing of vaults, safe deposits and other depositories. At the time
of sealing, persons who previously had access to the sealed depositories
shoula be warned about the consequences of breaking the seal. Individuals
shoula be desirnated to be responsible for the safety of the seal, and the
keys should be seized by Military Government Officers.
B. Physical seizure und impounding. Physical seizure and impounding of
records should be ordered enly in those instances where the material is of
immediute use to Military Government or where there is danger of destruetion
of important documents, such as records of the War, Navy and Munitions Minis-
tries, certain banking and insurance records, etc.
In general, however, Military Government snould avoid removing records
and archives, except for reasons of safety. when certain files or papers are
of outstanding inturest, they should be reproduced by microfilm or other
mechunical method, and the r produetion should be sent to headquarters, to be
forwarded to the interested offices or agencies. Adequate supplies of repro-
ducing equipment and materials should be made available for this purpose.
As a general procautionury measure, inventories of documents, catalogues,
indices to files, and other finding aids should be promptly seized, even when
the key records are not removed.
. Military custody of offices and buildings containing key records.
Certain offices and buildings containing key records can be closed and placed

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
under military custody, providing that the structure is in reasonably good
condition. This method may prove to be the most practicable one for safeguard-
ing the more important records of the Ministries and prefectural offices.
When a building is closed, caretakers and other personnel should not be per-
mitted to remain there, and telephones should be disconnected.
When records belonging to private organizations are seized, impounded,
or placed unuer military custody, the announcenent should be made that they
will be held until further notice, since the utility of such records, or the
period of time during which they will be needed, cannot be deternined in
advance.
When the operation or a lusiness or other organization is to be continued
or reestablished, the use of certain records will be indispensable. Although
every effort should be made to minimize interference with the use of such
records, the considerations enunerateu above requiring the preservation of
records should be regardod as paramount. Eventually, when records previously
seized, impounded, sealed or pleced unaer military custody are to be released,
a careful scrutiny and screening should preceed the lifting of the safeguard-
ing measure.
D. Issuance of an Ordinance. For the general preservation of key records,
it is recommended that, inmediately on occupation of Japan, an ordinance be
promulgated pronibiting, under severe penalties, the destruction, removal,
supression and damaging of records of public and seni-public agencies, of
business enterprises and private organizations. The penalties should be
appropriate, more severe than the penalties imposed by Japanese law.

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
The ordinance should: (1) declare that violation of the prohibition is
an act of sabotage, to be punished accordingly; (2) list broad categories of
records included in the prohibition; (3) fix responsibility of individuals
.concerned in, or having e knowledge of, removal of records from their proper
place since December 7, 1941; (4) ecify what constitutes an offense and
the punishment therefor; and (5) continue in effect existing Japanese law in
regard to maintunance, preservation and use of records.
E. Other Measures. Limitations of tine, storage space, and available
Military Government personnel for safeguarding records may require more flex-
ible methods of safeguarding certain public records (such as civil and commer-
cial registers, certain court records, utc.,, and less important business
records. In some instances, it may be practicable for Military Government to
hold Japanese nationals personally liable for records and files under their
care and supurvision. These indiviuunls should be personally warned of
their responsibility, direetly or through local officials; in other instances,
espocially in smaller localities, it may prove advisable to call a meeting
of the chiefs of sections handling records and of the leaders of public
aguncies and businees units, in order to impress upon them the personal
responsibility they huve ror the recoris protected under the ordinance.
The importance of safegur rding key records should be brought to the
attention of all militury persounel in the field, se that the work of the
Military Government authorities may be facilitated. No records should be
removed without proper authority.

IV. RECOMMENDATIONS IN SPECIAL FIELDS
A. Japanese Filing Practices and Mechanization of Records.
1. Filing Systems. According to information supplied by individuals
formerly engaged in business in Japan, Japanese filing systems, both public
and private, are similar to those in the United States. Correspondence,
invoices, records of sales, statistical reports, memoranda, personnel
records, documents, etc. are generally kept in folders wherever this is
practicable. The folders are kept in file cabinets, either in "alphabet-
ical" order or by Japanese character (KNJI). If the "alphabet" systen
is used, files may be labeled either in Honaji or in kana. When kana is
umployod, the order will follow the sequenee commonly used, i. e., "a", "i",
"e", "o", "ka", "ki", "ku", "ke", "ko", etc.
Precice information us to how a filing system would be set up with Kanji
as a basis is lucking. The folders might be filed according to number of
strokes in the characters or else the actual pronunciation of the characters
might be the basis. In the latter case, the order followed would be similar
to that of a file basud on komaji or kana, with characters having the same
pronuncintion arranged acsording to number of strokes.
In public apencies and in large privete concerns, a specinl section is
generally in charge of recoipt and dispatch of documents, editing and
dissemination of reports and maintenance of records. Although the extent of
centrelization mey vary with ench agency or concern, in general it appears
that, with the oxception of seeret or confidential documents, records of a
pelicy nature, statistieal roports and copies of memorands to the Minister
or Governor of the publie ugeney, or to the head of the concern would be
kept in these central files.

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.97)
-8 -
2. Mechanization of Japanese hecords. The trend toward mechanization
of Japanese records becane quite pronounced aiter 1936. Several government
agencies installed business machine systens and other agencies where such
machines were already in use considerably increased the amount of data and
records kept by machine. in outstanding example of the trend among govern-
ment agencies is the Bureau of Statistics which spent over $80,0o0 for
equipment in 1939-40.
Private concerns, such as life insurence companies, banks and large
manufacturers were quick to adopt this improvenent in record keeping. In
1940 the Nippon Life Insurance Company in Osaka had 31 punches, 10 sorters,
7 tabulutors, 2 multipliers and various auxiliary machines in use.
For the period between l94l-l94o, no information is available concerning
the use of business m.chines. However, it is knowm that merican concerns
in Japan were carrying businuss mechines in stock when the war began and there
is little doubt that these machinus have been put to use. In addition, since
Pearl Horbor, the Jepanese may have succeeded in developing reasonably good
imitations of machines m de abroad.
One of the ugencies not using machines prior to the Pacific war but
believed to have instelled a system of mechanized records since then is the
Police Burenu (KElHo KYuku) in the Ministry of Home Affairs. Aecording to
representatives of merican firms, the Police Bureau was contemplating
instelling business m chines ror maintining records of the entire population,
which would show date of birth, birthpl ce, sex, aate of entering school,
types of school, dute of eligitility for military sorvice, branch of service
and special abilities. In Apponaix li there is listed the various public
a encies und private concerns mowm to te umploying business machines in
1041.

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
B. Records of Public Agencies.
Introduction
The importance of government records to Military Government for edmin-
istrative and other purposes cannot be overemphasized. Here, as in other
fields, the Japanese were quick to adapt themselves to'estern ways, and
Military Government officers will find that Japanese public records are
kept systematically, each office in the Ministry in the government main-
taining its own files =nd transmitting copies of its records to a section
in the Secretariat or the General Affairs Bureau. These sections are
variously named "Documents Section" (BUNSHO KA), "General Affairs Section"
(SOMU KA) or "Secretariat Section" (HISHO KA). In general, the files of
the offices in the above category will yield documents and other informa-
tion relating to nationa! pelicies and national orgenization.
Because of the intensity of centralized control, the administrative
organization of the prefcctural and municipal offices is patterned after
that of the Finistries in Tokyo. In the prefectural government, the
"Secretariat" (KANBO) is usually in charge of documents, files and the
compilation of statistics for the area under its jurisdiction. Other
useful material may be found in the files of the other prefectural depart-
ments, namely the General Affairs Department (SoMUBU), Police Department
(KEISATSU BU), Economics Department (KEIZAI BU), and the Public Torks
Department (DOBOKU BU). These departments perform the administrative
functions of government on matters handed. down by the various Ministries
to the Regional Councils and thence to the Prefecture or KEN government.
Receuse of the small degree of autonomy exercised by the prefectures up
to 1944, a good deal of information pertaining to local administration

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
may be obtained from the files of the Minietries in Tokyo to which, pre-
sumably, periodic reports and memoranda concerning questions of policy
were submitted. After June 1944, & large measure of authority was dele-
gated to the Prefectural Governors (KENCHIJI) by the Ministries of Home
Affairs, Education, elfare, Agriculture and Commerce, Munitions and
Transportation. It may be assumed, therefore, that the files of the of-
fices of the above Ministries will not prove rich sources of information
on local matters arising after June 1944.
According to Japanese pres and radio reports, certain offices in the
Japanese government performing important functions have recently been
abolished. In such cases, wher the date of "abolition" occurred since
May 1945, and where it is believed that the offices in question were
incorporated into other governmental units, it is suggested, in the ab-.
sence of more complete information concerning the actual disposition of
the functions and records of these offices, that Military Government
Officers proceed on the assumption that the function is still being per-
formed at the same location and/or that the records have not yet been
removed. In the event such a removal did take place, a knowledge of
where the records were kept originally may prove helpful in locating
them.

C. Commercial and Industrial Records
1. Quasi-governmental Agencies. In addition to the official records
and files maintained by the government offices described in the preceding
section, a large volume of information concerning commercial activities
and industrial production is to be found in the offices of the numerous
quasi-governmental corporations,companies and associations established fer
the purpose of developing and controlling facilities and materials
required for the expansion of strategic industries. These organizations
are divided into the following categories:
a.
Control Societies (Tōsei-kai) and Control Associations (Tōsei-
1/
kumiai). The Control Societies and Control Associations, through their
control of production and distribution of the products of their respect-
ive industries, have played an important part in carrying out Japan's
war production program. Their records will aid Military Government
materially in establishing its. own production controls.
The major control societies maintain heaiquarter offices in Tokyo,
with branch offices in the eight regions and sub-branches in the prefect-
ures where their enterprises are centered. Gaps in available information,
and constant additions, reorganizations and mergers have made it impossi-
ble to compile a complete or up-to-date list of the societies and
associations and their location. To the extent that such-information
has been available in material translated from Japanese sources, however,
it has been included in Appendix A.
The Tōsei-kumiai are subordinate to the Tōsei-kai, but closely parallel
in function und organization. For & detailed discussion, see Civil
Affairs Guide, hdministration of Production Programs in Japan.

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.99)
-27 -
b. National Policy Companies (Kokusaku Kaisha), Special Companies,
(Tokushu Kaisha), Control Companies (Tōsei Kaisha), and Eidans. The first
two of this group are devoted to the development of certain regions and
industries; the control companies were established by specific ministerial
decree for the purpose of controlling production, acquisition of raw
materials and distribution; and the corporations established by the
government under the Eidan system are government-controlled agencies
engaged in activities which are frequently of an uneconomic character
but essential to the war effort. ell over 6oo of these various types of
organizations existed in 1943 but were reorganized subsequently. 
partial list is given in Appendix D.
The records of the more important of these companies should be
placed under military custody, or seized and impounded--that is, the
banks and other financial institutions, those companies concerned with
war production industries, and those enguged in regional and colonial
development.
(1) The Japan Electric Generation and Transmission Company
(hippon Hassōden K.k.). This government-controlled monopoly was set up
to unify electric power generation and transmission, Its records will
supply detailed informution concerning capecity and production of each
generating plant in Japan, together with fuel consumed in thermal plants
and sales to the regional special distribution companies set up in each
The operations of the
company are carried on by six divisions (bu,. One of these, dealing with
confidentiul records, investigations and auditing is under the direct
supervision of the president.

no list of Munitions Companies is vailable, the records of the War,

devoted to Japanese war production 1 will be useful in identifying and
locating the more important wer plants.
b. Other industries. According to Jupanese law, traders,

for ten years (Commercia1 Code, Suctions 36, 143, 147).
The preservation
of these records should be covered by the general ordinance issued by
Military Government.
D. Bunking Records.
Because of the important part played by the Jupanese banks in the
cconomic penetration of the continent cud the Pacific islands, in financ-
ing the war, and in ueveloping war industries both in Japan and occupied
arens, their records will be of primery value to Militory Government.
Immediately upon occupation of J pan, Lilit ry Government should: (1)
temporarily close the banks, (2) block 1l accounts, nd (3) seal vaults
and safe deposits. The bank buildings should be placed under military
custody and should be closed to all personnel except those assigned by
Military Government to special tasks.

F. Agricultural Records.
The preservation of key gricultural records should be covered by
general ordinance. The Japanese people should be acquainted, moreover,
through the press und the radio, with the consequences of infringement
of this order--that in the absence of records giving.a basis for assass-
ment of reasonable delivery quotas, Military Government may be compelled
to resort to drastic requisitioning of agricultural products, irrespective
of other considerations.

1
kumiai), their addresses, and registers of officers and members; and (2) an
additional and incomplete list of control societies and control associations
to which reference has been made from time to time in translations of Japanese

are not now available. To facilitate reference to the lists included in the

In order to suve time, translations prepared and duplicated by the Inter-
depurtmental Committee for the hequisition of Foreign Publications have
been utilized as issued. In many instances the Romaji equivalents for the
kanji are not accurate and more frequently the translations are either
incomplet: or inconsistent.
It should be noted that most of the material included in this appendix has
been translated fron the 1943 Control Socicty Year Book (Toseikai Nenkan);
it is understood that the information contained therein covered the period
prior to Septomber 1942. Until the 1944 or 1945 edition of the Toseikai
Nenkan is obtained by Military Government Officers in the field, however,
the information contained in these translations may be of some assistance to
Military Government in planning their activitics along these lines.

A I DE-ME M O I RE
The French Delegate General to the Levant has
just been informed by the Minister of the United.
Stetes at Beyrouth that the latter had, in concert
with his English, Soviet end Chinese colleagues,
requested of the Consulate of Soain, which had them
() 894.41+/
in its keeping, and had obtained from it, the handing
over of the Japenese diplomatic and consular archives.
From inrormation given by Mr. Wadsworth to General
Beynet, it apmears that the American, English, Soviet
and Chinese Renresentstives at Beyrouth ere preparing
to request of the sequestrator, in addition, the
handing over of all property for which, in Syria and
Lebanon, the ciolomatic and consular authorities of
Japan are resoonsible.
The Freneh Government can but express its surprise
that such measures have been taken without previous
consultation with its Representative in the Levant.
It deems it desirable that instructions be sent to Hr.
Wadsworth to the effect that the Representative of
France be immediat ly mede a party to the negotiations
of the Representatives of the Powers.
Speaking more generally, the French Government would
desire that the American Government be good enough to
inform

255
-2-
inform it of its intentions as regards the disposition
of Japanese public and private property situated
outside Japan. It would like to participate in the
measures taken in the non-belligerent countries with
regard to such oroperty.

abroad.

NEW YORK TIMES, SATURDAY, SEPT. 22, 1945; P.10.
[From Late Edition of Yesterday's Times.]
JAPANESE PUZZLE SWEDES
Report Order to Close Legation
and an Addition to Staff
Special to Tiz New York Times.
STOCKHOLM, Sweden, Sept. 20
—The Japanese embassy here has
received orders to dissolve itself
"because that is the United States
wish." On the other hand, the en-
voy, Suemasa Okamoto, who has
the rank of Minister, has been ad-
vised of the immediate addition to
his staff of a councillor of embassy,
and his naval attaché, Comdr. Iori
Mishina, has been promoted to
captain. These statements are
puzzling to Stockholm diplomats.
It has long been expected here
that Gen. Douglas MacArthur
would order the disbandment of
Japanese foreign diplomatic mis-
sions but the fact that the Japa-
nese have taken no steps to liqui-
date the legation and the report of
the enlargement of the staff would
seem to indicate to diplomats here
that new orders had since been re-
ceived from Washington.

SUEJECT:
French participation in seizure of Japanese
archives.
The matter of non-French participation in obtain-
ing the Japanese archives in Syria, mentioned in the French
1E
Aide-Memoire #771, is not something that Wadsworth has done
on his own. The originel inetruction, with regard to the
seizure of these archives, did NOT include the French but
4
only the US, UK, USSR and Jhina, and this telegram was drafted O
by Assistant Secretary Holmes at the request of, and with the
enproval of, Assistant Secretary Dunn.
Subsequently, the matter of French participation in
France was brought out by telegrems from Peris, and the matter
was referred to EUR, Mr. Matthews. A telegrem was prepared
by WE, Mr. Bonbright, inviting the French to participate in
obteining the archives in France.
To date, the French have NOT been invited to partici
pate with the Big Four in obtaining the archivee, and if they
ere invited, the whole question will be broken open with regard
to inviting the participation of all povers having interests
in the Far East, such as the Dutch, Australians, New Zealanders--.
I em ettaching copies of the circular telegrem of
August 14, 8 n.m. and the Denartment telegrem #4244 to Faris.
For your further information, the whole matter remains
: cademic until General MacArthur issues instructions to the Japa-
nese about the matter.

U.S. URGENT
CIRCULAR
TO ALL AMERICAN DIPLOMATIC MISSIONS
Immediately upon announcement of Japanese surrender
you should, on behalf of United States, Great Britain,
Soviet Union and China, request of FONOFF and protecting
power in belligerent countries custody of Japanese
archives and property before they can be destroyed. You
should request your British, Russian and Chinese colleagues
where present to associate themselves with this request.
Department is communicating with British, Russian
and Chinese Governments requesting them to issue appro-
priate instructions to their diplomatic representatives.
Upon surrender the Japanese Government will be in-
structed to order its missions in neutral countries to
14 360
relinquish diplomatic and consular property and archives
to custody of Allied powers. Japanese Government will be
instructed also to authorize governments protecting its
interests to relinquish property and archives in belligerent
countries.

II. LATERIAL IN JAPANESE.
The following is a summary of the miscellaneous material im
Japanese; it should be noted that Item 36(a), code book, is being
photostated at the present time:
1. Orficial Japanese gasette called Kanpo--a general report with
statistics and information on economic, industrial, and mili-
tary affairs. Among its contents are "Chokumei" (military cath);
military instructions; the attitude a soldier should have temesd
the Holy Spirit; the 1941 Army census and pensions; list of high
ranking Army officers traveling on official business to Framee,
Germany, Indo-China, Italy, Russia, and China; names of import-
ant businessmen, agriculturists, professors, eto.; informatiom
on foreign and domestic trade; weather statisties; individual
reports on persons killed in action during the war between Chime
and Japan; individual promotion reports, August 194l; repert es
a Japanese airbase in Indo-China, with instructions on its pretee-
tion; the military idea of how to control and take care of fereigm-
ers in Japanese-occupied areas; economic reports (agrioulture, im
dustry, etc.); information about Japanese living in Califermie
with addresses; guide for fishing in Japan; ships' records; forms
for people living outside Japan; market reports; dated 1941.
2.
Same as No. 1 (different issue of same gazette with similar oom-
tents).
3. Discussion, undated, of laws of Republic of Panama:
(a) Regarding discrimination; states that the 1938 law stepped
immigration of Japanese, Chinese, Hindus, Armeniams, Tarks,
and colored people; that this discrimination is taking place
in a so-called peaceful country; presents an unnamed indivi-
dual's point of view regarding this discrimination and the
increasingly unfavorable attitude toward the Japanese; re-
ports the stop ing of Japanese ships transiting the Caml
and prohibition of fishing in Panama.
(b) Regarding property buying, etc., especially as they affeet
Japanese residing in Panama.
(c) Regarding marriage of Panamanians by foreigners.
4.
Small memorandum book printed in 194l giving important dates,
such as holidays, the Emperor's birthday and those of his family,
addresses of Japanese diplomatic and consular missions, list of
ambassadors to Japan from other countries, money values of vari-
ous countries, and metric measurements. There is an entry im
March 1942 with names of Japanese and regarding a message to get
something ready. (This seems interesting but could not be fur-
ther explained.)

TEXT OF A TELEGRAM FROM BRITISH EMBASSY, WASHINGTON
TO FOREIGN OFFICE. LONDON. DATED S0ptember 14, 1945.
State Department have now issued a circular telegram
to United States representatives abroad informing them
that MacArthur has been direc ted to ins truct the Japanese
Government to order its representatives abroad to deliver
XR
up diplomatic and consular property and archives to the
701.9400
representatives of the Four Powers. Implication is that
representatives can make no progress until such orders
are issued unless government of the country to which they
are accredited and the Japanese officials in charge or the
representetive of the protecting power agree.
I understand that MacArthur has sent a reply to the
directive which objects to the procedure suggested in it.
I am trying to obtain further information about this.
State Department hope that when the Japanese Govern-
ment issues the appropriate orders even states which were
at war with Japan will agree to deliver up the archives
and property to the representa ti ves of the Four Powers
but if some states wish to take custody themselves, the
United States Government wo uld be content with permission
to examine the archives.
VE

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.94)
NOV5 1945

AIDE MEMOIRE
1945
His Majesty's Governnent in the United
Kingdom consider that access to official and non-
official records and archives in Japan, and to those
held outside Japan by Japanese authorities and persons,
will be of assistance to the Allied authorities in
connection with:-
(a) the obtaining of full information about
894.414/10-1245
Allied prisoners-of-war and civilian internees
and the investigation of their treatment during
custody;
(b)
the identification and prosecution of war
criminals;
(c)
the control of military anu inaustrial
disarmament;
(d) the idertification an disposal, in connection
with restitution and reparation, of United
Nations aná Japunese assets;
(e) the control o: political, financial and economic
activities and the dissolut ion of militarist
organisations;
y
(f)
 the acquisition of information about advances
OCT
made by Japanese science and industry;
FILED
1947
war;
(n)/
OCT 1 6 19450
EMS
DEPARTMENT OF ST

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.99)
267
(n)
the effect during the war of Allied
military operations, economic warfare,
propaganda, etc., on Japan.
2.
Of the se objectives the most urgent are
the tracing of Allied prisoners-of-war and the
identification of war criminals, since there is
every reason to expect that the Japanese, unless
speedily prevented, w ill destroy the relevant
records.
Arrangements have already been made for
the collection and preservation of records relating
to prisoners-of-war and their treatment in South
East Asia Command ano in the United States theatres.
It may be assumed, for South East Asia Command, and
probably also for the United States theatres, that
the same arrangements will apply to records relating
to civilian internees. No specific arrangements on
these matters, however, have been made with the
Russian and Chinese commanders, although General
Wedemeyer was informed last June of the orders issued
in the United States theatres and may have been able
to have similar orders issued in China by the
Ceneralissimo. It seems desirable therefore to
take steps to ensure that:-
(a) Any orders issued on this matter are
comprehensive and relate to civilian
internees as well as to prisoners-of-war.
(b)
Satisfactory arrangements are made for
the Russian and Chinese areas w hich do not
appear to have been covered hitherto.
3.1
2e

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.98)
3.
By "records and archives" is meant all
written, typescript, printed or punched papers,
parchment, or cards in any form, photographs or
phonographic records accumulated in the course of
the transaction of business or c onduct of affairs of
any kind (public, semi-public or private, lay,
military or civil) in any period, subsequently
preserved for reference in their original offices
or in special depositories together with all annexes
to them (including plans, drawings, maps, photographs
etc.) in any form.
4.
In order to prevent the destruction or con-
cealment of Japanese records and archives His Majesty's
Government in the United Kingdom consider that it is
urgently necessary that the Japenese Government should
be required to give orders to Japanese authorities
and persons at home and abroad for their preservation
in situ and their examina tion by the appropriate
Allied authority.
5.
His Majesty's Government in the United Kingdom
suggest that in the orders given by the Japanese Govern-
ment it should be provided that:-
(a)
the destruc tion, concealment, removal or
falsification of any records or archives
is forbidden under severe penalties;
(b)
all records and archives are held at the
disposal of the appropriate allied
authority for examination by, or surrender
to, him:
(c)./
3.

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.98)
299
(c)
assistance shall be given to the appropriate
Allied Authority by all Japanese authorities
and persons in regard to the e xamination of
records and archives.
5.
In order that the necessary examina tion may
be carried out the appropriate Allied authorities
(Supreme Commander Allied Forces in the Pacific in
metropolitan Japan, and the conmanders or
suc cessor
authorities in liberated territories) will have to
establish organizations, or designate personnel for
the purpose.
His Majesty's Government in the United
Kingdom would wish
to see one or more British expert
included in the staff employed on this task in
metropolitan Japan and His Majesty's Governments in
the Dominions and in India may wi sh to offer the
services of Dominion and Indian experts to take part
in the work.
6.
While much of the information secured by
these means will be of purely loc al interest, there
will be records and archives of wider value which it
is thought shoula be made availab le to a ll the Allied
Powers concerned.
His Majesty's Government in the
United Kingdom hope that arrangements may be made to
enable
the
appropriate Allied authorities to transmit
information derived from the records
and archives
(or where necessary reproductions of the originals) to
the Allied
Governments or to whatever organizations
may be set up to effect inter-Allied co-operation in
respect of Japan and the liberated territories.
4.
7.1

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.98)
270
7.
His Majesty's Government in the United
Kingdom hope that, if the United States Government
have not already done so, they will now give
Supreme Commander Allied Forces in the Pacific
instructions to issue to the Japanese authorities
orders to preserve Japanese records and archives and
make them available for examination and disposal.
They would also be glad to be informed whether the
United States Government concurs in the views of
His Majesty's Government in the United Kingdom
regarding the examination of the Japanese archives
and the release of the information so obtained to
the Allied Powers or the appropriate inter-Allied
organizations.
BRITISH EMBASSY,
WASHINGTON, D.C.

With reference to the Department's circular telegram

communications regarding Japanese archives ana property,
as well as to the Embassy's telegram No. 690 of Septem-
ber 10, 1945 on the same subject, I have the honor
to report that the Spanish Minister, having received
authorization from the roreign Office in Madrid, has
released to the Ecuadoran Ministry for Foreign Affairs
the Japanese archives and property which were left
in the custody of the panish Legation by the Japanese
Chargé d'Afraires in April 1942 upon his departure
894.414/10-154
from Ecuador.
12
As the Embassy indicated in its telegram under
reference, the Japanese archives and property which
were left in the custody of the protecting power in
rcuador consist only of sundry office supplies and
equipment, a few books in Japanese and two small folders
of correspondence in spanish relating to matters of
protocol. A copy and a translation of the inventory
of this property are enclosed. An officer of the
mbassy was allowed free access to the Japanese
property and archives by the Spanish Minister before
he delivered it to the cuadoran Ministry for Foreign
545
Affairs, and it was a certained that there was nothing
of importance among the items delivered, with the possi-
ble exception of the small office safe. It has been im-
possible to verify the contents, if any, of this safe,
since, according to the spanish Minister, the Japanese
Charge d'Affaires declared at the time of his departure
from Ecuador that the safe wes empty and left no combina-
tion.
This mbassy has not yet approached the Ecuadoran
authorities with a request for the custody of the Japanese
property and before taking any further action in the
matter, the Embassy would appreciate an instruction
from the Department indicating whether it desires that
894.414

for Foreign Affairs has expressed its desire to have ex-
clusive custody of the property, that the Ministry has
