Sir:
'I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt or
the Department's circular secret telegram of October 13,
1945, with respect to the disposition of Japanese Con-
sular and diplomatic arcnives. The Department states
3
that the Joint Chiefs of Staff have telegraphed
General MacArthur with regard to these Japanese arohives
stating that it is desired that unless for operational
reasons he may have objections, he proceed with the
issuance of the aporopriate instructions along the follow-
ing lines:
United States diplomatic missions are faced with a
difficult situation through the non-issuance of instruo-
tlons to Japanese missions concerning the disposal of
Consular archives and property. In neutral countries
the Japanese are acting as if no surrender had occurred.
In countries where Sweden and Switzerland are acting as
protecting powers access to the archives has not been
permitted. Accordingly it is urgently necessary to issue
instructions which will embody the following provisions:
1. In neutral countries Japanese missions
immediately should turn over diplomatic and
Consular archives and property to representa-
tives of the four allied powers. However, the
protection of nationals may be turned over to
Sweden and Switzerland since these countries
S
are acting as protecting powers for Japanese
elsewhere.
2. In countries where Sweden and
Switzarland are acting as protecting powers,
immediate instructions should be issued to
turn over physical custody of all diplomatic
and Consular property and archives to the
representatives of the four allied powers.
The protecting powers, however, should con-
tinue to exercise the routine functions of
protection of Japanese nationals.

Departmert ith regard to this matter of Japanese

had discussed this matter wit! my British, Soviet, and
Chinese colleagues and that all of them were of the
opinion which I expressed that the Mexican Government
would not agree to these arclives being turned over by

would only agree to the Mexican Government taking over

The British Ambassador came in to see me on
October ll and conveyed to me the substanoe of a tele-
gram which he has received from the Foreign Offioe in
London which is uated September 29. In this telegram
the Foreign Ofrice informs the British Ambassador that
Lord Halifax ir Washington has been informed that
General MacArthur has been directed by the Departmont
of State to instruct the Japanese Government to take
the necessary steps to deliver the Japanese diplomatic
and Consuler property and archives at posts abroad to
the representatives of the four powers. According to
the telegram, the British Ambassador here is informed
that he will be later iven information when the
Japanese Government has given the necessary instructions
to the protecting powers, and when these instruotions
are issued the hope is expressed that all states, in-
cluding those which were at war with Japan, will agree
to deliver the archives and property to the represonta-
tives of the four powers. The Foreign Office telegram
to the British Ambassador, however, goes on to state
that if some of the states may wish to take custody
themselves, the United States Government would be pre-
pared to accept this and to accept permission to examine
the archives.
The British Ambassador informed me that his Govern-
ment is in accord with this view and that should the
Mexican Government wish to take over the archives from
the Swedish Legation and retain custody, he will be in a
position under his instructions to agree with this: of
course, with the understanding that he and the repre-
sentatives of the other three powers will have the right

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.99)

AIR MAIL
-3 -
to have aocess to the archives.
It will be noted that the telegram whioh the
British Ambassador has received from the Foreign
Office is more olear and explicit than the Department's
ciroular telegram of October 13, 8 a.m., under referenee.
The Department's circular telegram under reference would
indicate that once the Japanese Government has issued the
appropriate instructions, the Swedish Legation in Mexico
would turn over these archives to the diplomatio repre-
sentatives of the United States, Great Britain, Russia,
and China. As I have already indicated in previous oom-
munications to the Department, I am confident that the
Mexican Government would not agree to this prooedure.
The Mexican Government considers itself as a belligorent.
It was one of the first, if not the first, of the other
American Republics to declare war on Japan. I know from
informal conversations in the Foreign Office here that
Mexican Government is prepared to collaborate with us
in the matter of Japanese archives just as it was pre-
pared to collaborate in matters concerning the German
archives and has collaborated so fully up to this time
with respect to German properties. The Mexican Govern-
ment, however, in the case of the German arohives in-
sisted thet it take them over and make available informa-
tion thereir to us. T is it has done. I am confident
that the Foreign Office here will take the same attitude
with regard to Japanese archives. In other words, that
it will be willing to take them over from the Swedish
Legation here and to make the contents available to the
four powers. I am equally confident, however, that it
will not agree to the Swedish Legation turning over the
arcnives to the British, Soviet, Chinese, and United
States representatives.
In view of the fact thit in the case of the Japanese
archives, as has been found to be the case with the German
archives, we will undcubtedly find that the signifioant
documents were destroyed when the representative Chiefs of
Mission left here on the declaration of war by Mexico. I
do not believe it is worth making a mountain out of a mole
hill and getting into any difficulties with the Mexioan
Government on this matter. I feel as my colleagues here
do that the attitude of the ∵exican Government on this
matter will be very defirite and that it will serve no
purpose whatever for us to endeevor to have the Swedish
Legetion turn these arcnives over to the four powers
directly.
While I have no information of a direot character,
I believe that we may safely assume that the attitude of
the other Governments of the American Republics will be
the same. My Soviet, British, and Chinese colleagues
are fully understanding of this position.
I would appreciate appropriate instructions from
the Department amending the circular telegram of
October 13, 8 a.m., in the sense that it will be satis-
factory, if the Mexican Government wishes to take these
aronives/

I venture the suggestion that similar instruetions

American Republios should the Governments in their
respective Republics indicate that they wish to
take over the arohives.

I have the honor to refer to the Department's
restricted circular telegram of October 13, 1945,
8 p.m., concerning possible arrangements for obtaining
access to Japanese archives in various countries in

Guatemala because there was no Japanese diplomatic

DEPARTMENT OF

DIVISION CF

T1846
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
SECRET
AMERICAN EMBASSY
México, D.F., México,
October 24, 1945.
Dear Carrigan:
I have just received the Department's circular secret
telegram of October 23, 5 p.m., of which I enclose a para-
phrase. You will note that this telegram leaves us
exactly where we were and that no account has been taken
of the recommendations which I have made in this matter.
According to paragraph "A" of this telegram, as Sweden
894.414/10-2445
is acting as the protecting power in Japanese interests
here, it means that the Japanese Government is telling
the Swedish Minister here to turn over these Japanese
archives "to representatives of the four Allied Powers
who have been instructed to receive them and to have
custody thereof".
I have informed the Department that I have been in
touch with my British, Chinese and Soviet colleagues here,
and they are in accord that the Mexican Government will
not permit the turning over of these archives by the Swedes
to the Four Powers. The Mexican Government will insist
that they be turned over to them. I am not taking any
action on this matter. I intend to call you on the tele-
phone with regard to this. If we carry through along the
line of this telegram in the American republics, I think
we are going to get into a snarl with all of them. It
doesn't seem to make any sense. I don't see any reason
why we should get into difficulties with the other American
republics, whose collaboration we want on major matters,
C
with regard to these archives when as a matter of fact these
S
archives will be found to contain nothing. I simply am
unable to understand why the Department will not take note
of considered recommendarions made by some of us in the
77
field, when obviously these are based on a knowledge of the
problem which we must have and which we are supposed to have.
I have e note from the Foreign Office this morning,
which I have not yet had time to study but which my people
tell me is reminiscent of the notes which the Mexicans
and
John Willard Carrigan, Esquire,
Department of State,
Washington, D. C.

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
AIR MAIL
we used to write to each other. It is probably the first
notification that we have that a new Foreign Minister is
here. The note complains, as I understand it, that we
have not been taking Mexico into account in a lot of
things although she was an Ally in the war and a member
of the United Nations.
In my opinion, we should immediately tell our
Chiefs of Mission in the American republics that we
can inform the appropriate Foreign Offices that we have
no objection to the protecting power turning over these
archives to the Mexican Government, or to the Governments
of the other American republics, but that the Four Powers
should nave access to them together with the respectiva
Covernnents. If we don't do this, we are simply going
to make a lot of trouble without any reason and without
it making any sense.
With all ood wishes,
Cordielly and sincerely yours,
Enclosure:
Paraphrase of Circular Secret
telegram of Cctober 23, 5.p.m.
from the Department.

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
No. 1220
Asunci on, Paraguay, October 24, 1945
Subject: Japanese diplomatic and consular archives in
Paraguay.
UNRESTRICTED
OF FOREIGN A
1945
The Honorable,
The Secretary of State,
Washington,D.C.
Sir:
1
I have the honor to refer to the Department's various telegraphic
instructions regarding the disposition of the archives and property of
the Japanese Government in Paraguay and to report the result of the
investigations made by this Embassy to date.
894.414/10-2445
The Spanish Chargé d' Affaires was consulted on May 21, 1945 and
stated that he had instructions to tum all Japanese property over to
n)
the Swedish diplomatic representative, and that, as there was no such
representative in Paraguay, he had communicated with the Swedish
Minister in Buenos Aires. The Chargé d' Affaires stated that the
Japanese Government property consisted principally of a couple of flags,
as the correspondence had been burned by the Japanese diplomatic officials
before they left this country.
The Ministry of Foreign Relations was also consulted but was not
aware of any Japanese archives which have been left here.
1
There is enclosed a copy of an informal memorandum received today
by First Secretary Reed from Under Secretary of Foreign Relations Bruno
Alfonso Campos, a translation of which follows herewith:
"Memorandum
"The Japanese diplomatic representation was presided over by Baron
Shu Tomii, with residence in Buenos Aires, who was accredited to the
Government of Paraguay on November 26, 1941.
"The Legation in Asuncion was in charge of Mr. Tomiya Koseki, who
carried on the functi ons of Commercial Attache, Chief of the Legation
Chancellery and came to Paraguay at the beginning of the year 1940,
leaving the country in the first months of the year 1942 because of the
CS/VJ
rupture of diplomatic relations with the Japanese Empire. Mr. Koseki
left for Buenos Aires before his German and Italian colleagues. Leaving
no archives or documents."
Respectfully yours,
Enclosure:
(Listed on following page)

possession of archives it might create suspicion. He
looked rather pointedly at the Soviet Minister. ThE
Soviet Minister madc no objection. It is clear, howover,
that later will await specific instructions from his
covernment before he agrees officially to anything. I
would appreciate the Department's further guidance in the
matter.
WILEY
JMS

ICV 1 1945
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: October 26, 1945
SUBJECT:
Press Announcement of General MacArthur's Order to the
Japanese to Direct the Turning Over of Property and
Archives Abroad to Representatives of the Four Powers
PARTICIPANTS:
Sefñor Don Arturo Bascufan, Chargé d'Affaires ad interim,
Chilean Embassy
NWC - Mr. Flack
COPIES TO:
MR. BRADEN
FC
OCT 2 9 1945
American Embassy, Santiago, Chile.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
The Chargé d'Affaires ad interim showed me the clipping
1C-2645
from the press on this subject and asked what information
the Department might have with regard to it.
I discussed the matter with Mr. Cherp in FC, who informed
me that circulars had been sent to the field, October 23,
5 p.m. and October 25, 8 a.m., which would reach us in due
course, informing our officers that representatives of the
four powers would consult and take over Japanese property
and archives which the protecting powers should turn over to
them intact. Mr. Cherp said at the same time the protecting
4R701.9466
power, that is Switzerland or Sweden as the case may be,
CS
would continue protection of individual Japanese.
/L
I explained this to the Chargé and in reply to his
inquiry as to how the powers might implement this, I said
m
that they would probably set up a committee for the purpose.
in the apitals tirer represented. He said that Soviet
Russia had no reprosentative at the present time in Santiago
and I mentioned that in that case the three powers would
894.414
no doubt consult with regard to the necessary steps. I
added that on the basis of the information I had, in all
probability the archives would remain in the building
where they had been previously under the charge of the
110-2645
protecting power and that they would be henceforth at the
46
disposition of the four powers.
The Chilean

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The Chilean Chargé intimated that he would like to
have any additional information which I might appropriately
give him on this subject and I told him I would be very
glad to supplement the foregoing information if I could
do so in the near future. He desired this information for
communication to his Government but nnot raise any
objection about the procedure which had been outlined.
